<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> At the critical weight of the regular honeycomb lattice, the total weight of self-avoiding paths crossing a strip of height <i>N</i> is comparable to <i>N</i><sup>−1/4</sup>. The first horizontal-displacement moment of return paths is comparable to <i>N</i><sup>3/4</sup>.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-strip)): For every integer $N\ge1$, all sums in <a href="#eq:01-strip-quantities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:01-strip-quantities">[eq:01-strip-quantities]</a> are finite. With constants independent of $N$, $$\label{eq:01-strip-result} c\mathcal A_N+\mathcal B_N=1,\qquad m_{N+1}-m_N\asymp\mathcal B_N,\qquad m_N\asymp N^{3/4},\qquad \mathcal B_N\asymp N^{-1/4}.$$ Moreover, $\mathcal B_N$ is nonincreasing in $N$.
  - Explanation: The critical honeycomb strip has finite unnormalized masses and displacement moment, with uniform bounded-factor powers and monotone bridge mass.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-boundary-flux)): Let $D$ be a finite simply connected union of triangles with simple polygonal boundary, and let $a$ be a boundary port. If $W(\gamma)$ is the total signed turning of a path, measured from its inward initial direction to its outward final direction, then $$\label{eq:01-boundary-identity} \sum_{b\in\partial D}\ \sum_{\gamma:a\to b} \rho^{\|\gamma\|}e^{itW(\gamma)}=1.$$ If $D$ is convex, its unsigned boundary partition sum is at most $1/c$.
  - Explanation: Local mass conservation and cancellation of stopped collisions give a signed boundary identity and a convex-domain unsigned bound.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-local-identities)): Writing $R_i$ for action in slots $i,i+1$, one has $$\begin{aligned} R_2(y)R_1(x+y)R_2(x)&=R_1(x)R_2(x+y)R_1(y), \label{eq:01-braid}\\ R(s\lambda)&=S_sS_s^{\mathsf t},\qquad s=2,3, \label{eq:01-factorization}\\ R_2(x)R_1(x+s\lambda)(I\otimes S_s) &=(S_s\otimes I)\widehat R_s(x), \label{eq:01-splitting}\\ R(x)R(-x)&=I.\label{eq:01-unitarity} \end{aligned}$$ Here $\widehat R_2(x)=R(x+\lambda)$ and $\widehat R_3(x)$ is the one-slot identity. These are identities of rational functions. They can be specialized wherever the relevant sides are regular, including at removable singularities; an individual operator at a genuine pole is not assigned a finite value.
  - Explanation: The diagram operators satisfy braid, factorization, splitting and unitarity identities as rational functions, subject to regular specialization.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-transfer-decay)): If every $u_i$ belongs to $\{0,\lambda,2\lambda,3\lambda\}$, then $T_N$ restricted to vectors with zero empty coordinate and $T_N^{(1)}$ have spectral radius strictly less than one. The same holds for reversed columns and in a complex neighborhood of each such parameter list, with absolute values of tile weights used to control absolute convergence.* *There is consequently a unique stationary vacuum vector $P_N$ with empty coordinate one. Its entries are rational in the $e^{iu_i}$, and regular at every parameter list just specified.
  - Explanation: The nonempty vacuum and source transfers decay at the specified row lists, giving a unique normalized stationary vector with rational continuation.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-reductions)): The function $p_N$ is a symmetric polynomial of degree at most $N-1$ in each argument. Its leading coefficient in any argument is $p_{N-1}$ of the remaining arguments, and $$\label{eq:01-scalar-flat} p_N(Y,c)=p_{N-1}(Y)\prod_{z\in Y}(z+C).$$ For $f_j=\cos(w+j\lambda)$, $$\begin{aligned} p(Y,f_0,f_6) &=p(Y)(f_0+f_6+\rho) \prod_{z\in Y}(z-f_{-6})(z-f_{12}), \label{eq:01-scalar-delete}\\ p(Y,f_{-2},f_2) &=p(Y,f_0)(f_{-2}+f_2+\rho) \prod_{z\in Y}(z+f_0). \label{eq:01-scalar-merge} \end{aligned}$$ Here the subscript of $p$ is its number of arguments. Both formulas also hold with all shifts negated.
  - Explanation: The scalar Pfaffian becomes a symmetric polynomial with bounded degree and explicit flat, deletion and merge reductions.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-vacuum-degree)): The vector $W_N=p_NP_N$ is Laurent polynomial in $e^{iu_1},\ldots,e^{iu_N}$, with each exponent between $-(2N-2)$ and $2N-2$. In the last variable $y=u_N$, $b=a(y)$, its last-slot-vacant part is a polynomial in $b$ of degree at most $N-1$ with leading coefficient $W_{N-1}$. Its last-slot-occupied part is $\sin y$ times a polynomial in $b$ of degree at most $N-2$. Set $W_0=1$.
  - Explanation: Scalar normalization removes the vacuum poles and controls Laurent degree, including the separate vacant and occupied last-slot forms.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-arch-formula)): Let $\eta=2d-1$ and $$\label{eq:01-g-definition} g_X(z)=\frac{p_{N+1}(a_1,\ldots,a_N,z)}{p_N(a_1,\ldots,a_N)}.$$ Then $g_X$ is monic of degree $N$ for generic $X$, and $$\label{eq:01-arch-formula} m(X)=\sum_{i=1}^Na_i-\eta[z^{N-1}]g_X(z).$$ The right side is understood by continuation at removable singularities.
  - Explanation: The arch displacement moment is recovered from the subleading coefficient of the monic Pfaffian quotient, with removable values understood by continuation.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-moment-increments)): For homogeneous honeycomb strips, $c\mathcal A_N+\mathcal B_N=1$, the bridge mass is nonincreasing, and $m_{N+1}-m_N\asymp\mathcal B_N$ uniformly in $N\ge1$.
  - Explanation: A stepped-boundary flux identity compares moment increments with bridge mass uniformly in strip height.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-missing-harmonics)): Every sine harmonic of $\Phi_X$ whose frequency is divisible by four has coefficient zero. Equivalently, writing $h=b^2(1-b^2)=(1-\cos4w)/8$, there are polynomials such that $$\label{eq:01-projection} Q_X(b)=\mathcal P(h)+b\mathcal J(h)+b^2\mathcal E(h), \quad \deg\mathcal P,\deg\mathcal J\le N, \quad \deg\mathcal E\le N-1.$$
  - Explanation: Missing sine harmonics constrain the polynomial to the stated projection space.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-positive-integral)): The monic problem <a href="#eq:01-homogeneous-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:01-homogeneous-projection">[eq:01-homogeneous-projection]</a> has a unique solution, which is the limit of $g_X$ as all $a_i\to C$. Let $$\label{eq:01-omega} \omega(x)=\frac12\sqrt{\sqrt{\frac{1+x}{2x}}-1},\qquad 0<x<1,$$ and let $(y_1,\ldots,y_N)$ have density proportional to $$\label{eq:01-positive-law} \prod_{i=1}^N\omega(y_i) \prod_{i<j}\frac{(y_j-y_i)^2}{y_i+y_j}, \qquad 0<y_1<\cdots<y_N<1.$$ Then $$\label{eq:01-moment-integral} m_N=\frac\eta\pi\int_0^1 \left(1-\mathbb E\prod_{i=1}^N\frac{y_i-x}{y_i+x}\right) \frac{\omega(x)}x\,dx.$$
  - Explanation: A positive Cauchy moment determinant resolves the coincident-parameter limit and represents the arch moment by a positive probability integral.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-association)): Let $I\subset(0,\infty)$ be an interval, let $n\ge1$, and let $w_1,\ldots,w_n$ be positive smooth functions on $I$. Suppose $$\prod_{i=1}^n w_i(x_i)\prod_{i<j}K(x_i,x_j),\qquad K(x,y)=\frac{(y-x)^2}{x+y},\qquad x_1<\cdots<x_n,\quad x_i\in I,$$ has finite positive normalizing integral. Its probability law is positively associated: increasing integrable functions have nonnegative covariance whenever the covariance is defined. The assertion also holds after truncation to a shorter common interval. Consequently, a coordinatewise increasing likelihood ratio shifts the coordinates upwards in stochastic order, and a decreasing one shifts them downwards.
  - Explanation: The ordered positive pair kernel gives association and stochastic comparison under monotone likelihood ratios.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-laguerre)): For fixed $a>0$ and every integer $n\ge1$, $$\label{eq:01-normalizer} Z_n(a)=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\Gamma(a+i) \prod_{0\le i<j<n}\frac{j-i}{2a+i+j}.$$ There exist constants $K_a,C_a$, depending only on $a$, such that $\mathbb P_{n,a}(r_n\le K_an)$ is bounded below by a positive constant independent of $n$, and, with $D_k=ak+k(k-1)/2$, $$\label{eq:01-small-coordinate} \mathbb P_{n,a}(r_k<h) \le\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{C_a h n^2}{k^3}\right)^{D_k}\right\}, \qquad h>0,\quad 1\le k\le n.$$ In particular, $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb E_{n,1/2}\left(\sum_i r_i^{-1}\right)^{1/4} &\lesssim n^{1/2},\label{eq:01-reference-upper}\\ \mathbb E_{n,2}\sum_i r_i^{-1}&\lesssim n^2, \label{eq:01-reference-inverse}\\ \mathbb E_{n,1}r_1^{-1/4}&\gtrsim n^{1/2}. \label{eq:01-reference-lower} \end{aligned}$$ The constants in the last three bounds are absolute.
  - Explanation: The generalized Bures–Laguerre normalizer yields coordinate-tail and inverse-moment bounds.

          "exact_quote": "Writing $R_i$ for action in slots $i,i+1$, one has $$\\begin{aligned}\n R_2(y)R_1(x+y)R_2(x)&=R_1(x)R_2(x+y)R_1(y),\n       \\label{eq:01-braid}\\\\\n R(s\\lambda)&=S_sS_s^{\\mathsf t},\\qquad s=2,3,\n       \\label{eq:01-factorization}\\\\\n R_2(x)R_1(x+s\\lambda)(I\\otimes S_s)\n       &=(S_s\\otimes I)\\widehat R_s(x),\n       \\label{eq:01-splitting}\\\\\n R(x)R(-x)&=I.\\label{eq:01-unitarity}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $\\widehat R_2(x)=R(x+\\lambda)$ and $\\widehat R_3(x)$ is the one-slot identity. These are identities of rational functions. They can be specialized wherever the relevant sides are regular, including at removable singularities; an individual operator at a genuine pole is not assigned a finite value.",
          "source_id": "PM-237.11"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-transfer-decay",
        "exact_quote": "If every $u_i$ belongs to $\\{0,\\lambda,2\\lambda,3\\lambda\\}$, then $T_N$ restricted to vectors with zero empty coordinate and $T_N^{(1)}$ have spectral radius strictly less than one.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-vacuum-degree",
        "exact_quote": "The vector $W_N=p_NP_N$ is Laurent polynomial in $e^{iu_1},\\ldots,e^{iu_N}$, with each exponent between $-(2N-2)$ and $2N-2$.         "exact_quote": "Let $\\eta=2d-1$ and $$\\label{eq:01-g-definition}\n g_X(z)=\\frac{p_{N+1}(a_1,\\ldots,a_N,z)}{p_N(a_1,\\ldots,a_N)}.$$ Then $g_X$ is monic of degree $N$ for generic $X$, and $$\\label{eq:01-arch-formula}\n m(X)=\\sum_{i=1}^Na_i-\\eta[z^{N-1}]g_X(z).$$ The right side is understood by continuation at removable singularities.",

        "exact_quote": "The monic problem <a href=\"#eq:01-homogeneous-projection\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:01-homogeneous-projection\">[eq:01-homogeneous-projection]</a> has a unique solution, which is the limit of $g_X$ as all $a_i\\to C$. Let $$\\label{eq:01-omega}\n \\omega(x)=\\frac12\\sqrt{\\sqrt{\\frac{1+x}{2x}}-1},\\qquad 0<x<1,$$ and let $(y_1,\\ldots,y_N)$ have density proportional to $$\\label{eq:01-positive-law}\n \\prod_{i=1}^N\\omega(y_i)\n \\prod_{i<j}\\frac{(y_j-y_i)^2}{y_i+y_j},\n \\qquad 0<y_1<\\cdots<y_N<1.$$ Then $$\\label{eq:01-moment-integral}\n m_N=\\frac\\eta\\pi\\int_0^1\n  \\left(1-\\mathbb E\\prod_{i=1}^N\\frac{y_i-x}{y_i+x}\\right)\n  \\frac{\\omega(x)}x\\,dx.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-237.11"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-association",
        "exact_quote": "Let $I\\subset(0,\\infty)$ be an interval, let $n\\ge1$, and let $w_1,\\ldots,w_n$ be positive smooth functions on $I$. Suppose $$\\prod_{i=1}^n w_i(x_i)\\prod_{i<j}K(x_i,x_j),\\qquad\n K(x,y)=\\frac{(y-x)^2}{x+y},\\qquad x_1<\\cdots<x_n,\\quad x_i\\in I,$$ has finite positive normalizing integral. Its probability law is positively associated: increasing integrable functions have nonnegative covariance whenever the covariance is defined. The assertion also holds after truncation to a shorter common interval. Consequently, a coordinatewise increasing likelihood ratio shifts the coordinates upwards in stochastic order, and a decreasing one shifts them downwards.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-laguerre",
        "exact_quote": "For fixed $a>0$ and every integer $n\\ge1$, $$\\label{eq:01-normalizer}\n Z_n(a)=\\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\\Gamma(a+i)\n             \\prod_{0\\le i<j<n}\\frac{j-i}{2a+i+j}.$$ There exist constants $K_a,C_a$, depending only on $a$, such that $\\mathbb P_{n,a}(r_n\\le K_an)$ is bounded below by a positive constant independent of $n$, and, with $D_k=ak+k(k-1)/2$, $$\\label{eq:01-small-coordinate}\n \\mathbb P_{n,a}(r_k<h)\n \\le\\min\\left\\{1,\\left(\\frac{C_a h n^2}{k^3}\\right)^{D_k}\\right\\},\n \\qquad h>0,\\quad 1\\le k\\le n.$$ In particular, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb E_{n,1/2}\\left(\\sum_i r_i^{-1}\\right)^{1/4}\n       &\\lesssim n^{1/2},\\label{eq:01-reference-upper}\\\\\n \\mathbb E_{n,2}\\sum_i r_i^{-1}&\\lesssim n^2,\n       \\label{eq:01-reference-inverse}\\\\\n \\mathbb E_{n,1}r_1^{-1/4}&\\gtrsim n^{1/2}.\n       \\label{eq:01-reference-lower}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The constants in the last three bounds are absolute.",
        "source_id": "PM-237.11"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Boundary port** ([source](#eq:critical-weight)): A *port* is the midpoint of an edge on the boundary of a union of triangles.
- **Path length** ([source](#eq:critical-weight)): Its length $\|\gamma\|$ is the number of visited dual vertices; ports carry no weight.
- **Arch and bridge** ([source](#eq:01-strip-quantities)): A path returning to the bottom is called an *arch*; a path ending at the top is called a *bridge*.
- **Strip** ([source](#eq:01-strip-quantities)): Let $\mathcal S_N$ be the infinite horizontal strip of $N$ triangular bands, where $N\ge1$.
- **Strip observables** ([source](#eq:01-strip-quantities)): Define $$\label{eq:01-strip-quantities} \begin{split} K_N(k)&=Z_{\mathcal S_N}(0,k),\\ \mathcal A_N&=\sum_{k\ne0}K_N(k),\qquad \mathcal B_N=\sum_{b\text{ on the top}}Z_{\mathcal S_N}(0,b),\\ m_N&=\sum_{k\ge1}kK_N(k). \end{split}$$ The sums run over actual admissible boundary ports.
- **Scalar Pfaffian** ([source](#eq:01-pfaffian)): Define $$\label{eq:01-pfaffian} \begin{split} D(a,b)&=a^2+b^2+2dab-d^2,\\ p_N(a_1,\ldots,a_N) &=\prod_{i<j}\frac{D(a_i,a_j)}{a_i-a_j} \operatorname{Pf}\left[ \frac{(a_i-a_j)(a_i+a_j+\rho)}{D(a_i,a_j)} \right]. \end{split}$$ For odd $N$, augment the skew matrix by a last column of ones and its negative transpose; set $p_0=1$.
- **Source gluing pairing** ([source](#eq:01-moment-gluing)): Let $\operatorname{Glue}$ be the bilinear pairing of one-source states that is one when their occupancies agree and gluing joins the two sources into a single component with no loops, and zero otherwise.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-flux) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-flux): Conserve local complex mass and reverse stopped cycles. -> Local mass conservation and cancellation of stopped collisions give a signed boundary identity and a convex-domain unsigned bound.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-transfer-decay) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-transfer-decay): Use spanning components and finite-domain bounds to force transfer powers to vanish. -> The nonempty vacuum and source transfers decay at the specified row lists, giving a unique normalized stationary vector with rational continuation.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-reductions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-reductions): Clear Pfaffian denominators and use scalar reductions. -> The scalar Pfaffian becomes a symmetric polynomial with bounded degree and explicit flat, deletion and merge reductions.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-vacuum-degree) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-vacuum-degree): Interpolate the vacuum and prove stationarity by degree and parity. -> Scalar normalization removes the vacuum poles and controls Laurent degree, including the separate vacant and occupied last-slot forms.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-prop-arch-formula) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-arch-formula): Glue source resolvents and interpolate the arch coefficient. -> The arch displacement moment is recovered from the subleading coefficient of the monic Pfaffian quotient, with removable values understood by continuation.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-moment-increments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-moment-increments): Apply stepped-boundary flux and positive path decompositions. -> A stepped-boundary flux identity compares moment increments with bridge mass uniformly in strip height.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-integral) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-integral): Use harmonic projection and Cauchy moment determinant positivity. -> A positive Cauchy moment determinant resolves the coincident-parameter limit and represents the arch moment by a positive probability integral.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-thm-strip) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-laguerre-2): Compare associated positive measures with Bures–Laguerre laws and sum monotone increments. -> The critical honeycomb strip has finite unnormalized masses and displacement moment, with uniform bounded-factor powers and monotone bridge mass.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: The Cauchy determinant is positive on the ordered positive integration coordinates.
- **I2**: Arch and bridge quantities are unnormalized partition masses.
- **I3**: The restricted transfers have spectral radius strictly less than one at the specified parameter lists.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Interpret bridge weights as an endpoint probability distribution. -> Weights lack probability normalization.
- **A2**: Evaluate a local rational operator at a genuine pole. -> An undefined value is treated as finite.
- **A3**: Use transfer decay for unrestricted spectral parameters. -> Convergence of the source resolvent is assumed outside the established regime.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The Cauchy determinant calculation in the moment-matrix argument is a finite exact algebraic construction. Its finite checks do not certify the infinite-strip power law.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON x and y arrays of rational strings.
# PRECONDITIONS: equal lengths, 1 <= n <= 4, strictly ordered positive coordinates.
# OUTPUTS: exact reduced rational Cauchy determinant, numerator/denominator.
# TIME: O(n^2) rational operations; bit costs depend on input sizes.
# SPACE: O(n) rational coordinates.
# ARITHMETIC: exact python-flint fmpq; no floating point.
# FAILURE: reject malformed, unordered, nonpositive or oversized inputs.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq
obj = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
x = [fmpq(t) for t in obj['x']]
y = [fmpq(t) for t in obj['y']]
n = len(x)
if not (1 <= n <= 4 and len(y) == n):
    raise ValueError('dimension')
for a in (x, y):
    if any(t <= 0 for t in a) or any(a[i] >= a[i+1] for i in range(n-1)):
        raise ValueError('ordered positive coordinates required')
num = fmpq(1)
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(i+1, n):
        num *= (x[j]-x[i])*(y[j]-y[i])
den = fmpq(1)
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(n):
        den *= x[i]+y[j]
result = num/den
print(str(result.numerator)+'/'+str(result.denominator))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The local diagram identities and finite strip transfers provide mathematical contracts for symbolic lattice-model software; the paper supplies no software implementation. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The honeycomb path convention and positive integral law can guide simulations of the stated lattice model; simulation procedures and error guarantees are not supplied. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The coefficient tables, reduction identities and homogeneous projection provide source-bound checks for symbolic mathematical tools. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The proof constructs the homogeneous monic polynomial by a finite coefficient linear system, which supports an inferred symbolic algorithm for a fixed strip height. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Noncrossing partial pairings with vacancies and an exposed source strand give a finite representation of strip-cut connectivity. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The finite transfer matrices specify mathematical operations, but an accelerator layout, arithmetic format and device performance contract are ABSENT. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The boundary and transfer identities concern weighted lattice paths; a cryptographic primitive, adversarial model and security guarantee are ABSENT. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The positive law and moment estimates describe the stated interacting coordinates; a learning objective, estimator and generalization guarantee are ABSENT. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper solves polynomial coefficient constraints and compares probability laws; an optimisation objective and feasible decision problem are ABSENT. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Diagram exchange and column gluing are algebraic relations on lattice cut states; a distributed execution model and consistency guarantee are ABSENT. |
| other (critical lattice statistical mechanics) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Critical lattice statistical mechanics is the stated setting: the paper relates arch displacement moments, bridge mass and positive interacting-coordinate laws. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The strip power laws concern the regular honeycomb lattice at its critical path weight. Blocked use: ['Transfer the strip exponents to a different lattice or a noncritical path weight without a separate argument.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The path convention requires self-avoidance and boundary contact only at the endpoint ports. Blocked use: ['Apply the strip conclusions to paths that revisit a dual vertex or touch the boundary at interior points.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): Path length counts visited dual vertices, and ports carry no weight; changing the endpoint convention requires the corresponding fixed endpoint factor. Blocked use: ['Use ordinary edge-counting weights without reconciling the endpoint convention.']
- **L4** (hypothesis): The crossing and return observables are unnormalized masses, so their interpretation as probability laws requires normalization. Blocked use: ['Interpret individual bridge weights or the raw bridge mass as normalized probabilities.']
- **L5** (approximation): The strip theorem gives bounded-factor power laws; it does not identify an exact asymptotic prefactor or a limiting ratio. Blocked use: ['Replace the bounded-factor comparison by an asymptotic equality with a specified leading constant.']
- **L6** (edge_case): The height-zero bridge term is a convention and adds no strip path to the model. Blocked use: ['Treat the height-zero convention as a path-counting theorem for a degenerate strip.']
- **L7** (counterexample_scope): The displacement moment sums over all path lengths with strip height as its scale; fixed-length displacement and the conformal scaling limit concern different observables. Blocked use: ['Claim the fixed-length displacement conjecture or conformal scaling limit from the strip moment exponent.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The unsigned boundary partition bound requires convexity in the boundary-identity lemma. Blocked use: ['Apply that unsigned bound to arbitrary nonconvex domains solely from the stated boundary identity.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): The boundary identity is stated for a finite simply connected triangular domain with simple polygonal boundary and a boundary source port. Blocked use: ['Apply the identity directly to a multiply connected domain or an infinite domain without a separate limiting argument.']
- **L10** (edge_case): Rational local identities may be specialized at regular points and removable singularities, while a genuine pole has no assigned finite operator value. Blocked use: ['Evaluate an individual local operator at a genuine pole as a finite matrix.']
- **L11** (approximation): The transfer-decay argument works at fixed height and supplies no height-independent spectral-gap estimate. Blocked use: ['Infer a uniform contraction rate as strip height grows.']
- **L12** (edge_case): The arch formula is extended through removable singularities by continuation; a direct singular quotient evaluation is not justified. Blocked use: ['Substitute coincident parameters into an unreduced Pfaffian quotient without the established continuation.']
- **L13** (hypothesis): Association and monotone comparison require a finite positive normalizing integral for the ordered positive-weight law. Blocked use: ['Transfer the association conclusion to signed weights or to a density with no finite positive normalization.']
- **L14** (hypothesis): The Laguerre coordinate estimates are stated for fixed positive parameter, with constants depending on that parameter. Blocked use: ['Assume parameter-uniform tail constants when the Laguerre parameter varies.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 237 (`lean/docs/237.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Polynomial vacuum representations and bridge mass for honeycomb walks](../../preprints/Polynomial-vacuum-representations-and-bridge-mass-for-honeycomb-walks-September-26-2026/main.pdf)
> - [Renewal and changes of law for critical honeycomb walks](../../preprints/Renewal-and-changes-of-law-for-critical-honeycomb-walks-September-26-2026/main.pdf)
> - [Critical strip-crossing mass on the honeycomb lattice](../../preprints/Critical-strip-crossing-mass-on-the-honeycomb-lattice-September-26-2026/main.pdf)
> - [Critical honeycomb chords with prescribed boundary endpoints](../../preprints/Critical-honeycomb-chords-with-prescribed-boundary-endpoints-September-26-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The linked formalization establishes finiteness of the critical bridge measures used in the paper. For every strip height $h\ge1$, the total weight and first length moment of self-avoiding honeycomb bridges from a fixed initial port to a free terminal port are finite. The corresponding sums are also finite when the bridge is confined to the corridor of horizontal width $h(\log h)^2$, including the normalized first-moment sums.
>
> These are supporting summability statements. The paper's $h^{4/3+o(1)}$ mean-length law and its central-visit and hexagonal-chord exponents are outside them.
>
> The linked formalization proves existence of the infinite-length free energy for the critical honeycomb self-avoiding-walk partition function. For every starting vertex, force direction, and real force parameter, the logarithm of the length-$n$ partition function divided by $n$ converges to its stated free-energy limit.
>
> This selected result supplies the limiting free energy. It does not state the paper's small-force exponent, near-critical correlation scale, or the $3/4$ spatial and moment laws.
>
> For critical self-avoiding walks on the honeycomb lattice, the formalization proves that the total weight of paths crossing a strip of height $N$ is comparable to $N^{-1/4}$, while the first horizontal-displacement moment of return paths is comparable to $N^{3/4}$. It also proves finiteness of the strip sums, the exact arch–bridge balance identity, comparison of successive moment increments with bridge mass, and monotonicity of bridge mass. The comparison constants are uniform in the strip height.
>
> For the prescribed-boundary-endpoint companion, let $B_h$ be the total critical weight of strict bridges crossing a strip of integral height $h$, from one fixed bottom port to all compatible top ports, with $B_0=1$. The formalization proves
> $c(1+h)^{-1/4}\le B_h\le C(1+h)^{-1/4}$
> for all $h\ge0$ and fixed positive constants $c,C$. This includes finiteness of the path sum. The prescribed-endpoint length law, arch and strip mean laws, and remaining moment and renewal conclusions are outside this supporting statement.

Comparator source `CriticalStripMass.lean` (2506 bytes, sha256 `27aa8152c87d2034fe27f05b3cedb16287fbaf3311cd9fddfdfa187f6cbc8f0d`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

 

namespace CriticalStrip

noncomputable section
open scoped BigOperators
open Set MeasureTheory

def rho : ℝ := 1 / Real.sqrt (2 + Real.sqrt 2)
def c : ℝ := Real.cos (3 * Real.pi / 8)
def lambda : ℝ := Real.pi / 8
def d : ℝ := Real.cos (2 * lambda)
def C : ℝ := Real.cos lambda
def eta : ℝ := 2 * d - 1

abbrev Triangle := ℤ × ℕ × Bool

def up (i : ℤ) (j : ℕ) : Triangle := (i, j, true)
def down (i : ℤ) (j : ℕ) : Triangle := (i, j, false)

def upDownAdjacent (v w : Triangle) : Prop :=
  v.2.2 = true ∧ w.2.2 = false ∧
    ((v.2.1 = w.2.1 ∧ (w.1 = v.1 ∨ w.1 + 1 = v.1)) ∨
      (v.2.1 = w.2.1 + 1 ∧ v.1 = w.1))

def adjacent (v w : Triangle) : Prop := upDownAdjacent v w ∨ upDownAdjacent w v

 
def IsPath (N : ℕ) (finish : Triangle) (p : List Triangle) : Prop :=
  p.head? = some (up 0 0) ∧ p.getLast? = some finish ∧ p.Nodup ∧
  p.IsChain adjacent ∧ ∀ v ∈ p, v.2.1 < N

 
def archWeight (N : ℕ) (k : ℤ) (p : List Triangle) : ℝ := by
  classical
  exact if k ≠ 0 ∧ IsPath N (up k 0) p then rho ^ p.length else 0

 
def bridgeWeight (N : ℕ) (k : ℤ) (p : List Triangle) : ℝ := by
  classical
  exact if 0 < N ∧ IsPath N (down k (N - 1)) p then rho ^ p.length else 0

def K (N : ℕ) (k : ℤ) : ℝ := ∑' p, archWeight N k p
def bridgeKernel (N : ℕ) (k : ℤ) : ℝ := ∑' p, bridgeWeight N k p
def archMass (N : ℕ) : ℝ := ∑' k : ℤ, K N k
def bridgeMass (N : ℕ) : ℝ := ∑' k : ℤ, bridgeKernel N k
def moment (N : ℕ) : ℝ := ∑' k : ℕ, (k + 1 : ℝ) * K N (k + 1)

 
def StripSumsFinite (N : ℕ) : Prop :=
  (∀ k : ℤ, Summable (archWeight N k)) ∧
  (∀ k : ℤ, Summable (bridgeWeight N k)) ∧
  Summable (K N) ∧ Summable (bridgeKernel N) ∧
  Summable (fun k : ℕ => (k + 1 : ℝ) * K N (k + 1))

 
def Comparable (f g : ℕ → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∃ lo hi : ℝ, 0 < lo ∧ 0 < hi ∧
    ∀ N : ℕ, 1 ≤ N → lo * g N ≤ f N ∧ f N ≤ hi * g N

 
def CriticalStripMass : Prop :=
  (∀ N : ℕ, 1 ≤ N → StripSumsFinite N) ∧
  (∀ N : ℕ, 1 ≤ N → c * archMass N + bridgeMass N = 1) ∧
  Comparable (fun N => moment (N + 1) - moment N) bridgeMass ∧
  Comparable moment (fun N => (N : ℝ) ^ (3 / 4 : ℝ)) ∧
  Comparable bridgeMass (fun N => (N : ℝ) ^ (-(1 / 4) : ℝ)) ∧
  (∀ N : ℕ, 1 ≤ N → bridgeMass (N + 1) ≤ bridgeMass N)

 

theorem critical_strip_mass : CriticalStripMass := by
  sorry

end
end CriticalStrip

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


At the critical weight of the regular honeycomb lattice, the total weight of self-avoiding paths crossing a strip of height <i>N</i> is comparable to <i>N</i><sup>−1/4</sup>. The first horizontal-displacement moment of return paths is comparable to <i>N</i><sup>3/4</sup>.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

A self-avoiding path at its critical weight can make a long excursion without paying an exponential cost. A basic quantitative question is how much of its weight reaches the opposite side of a strip. On the regular honeycomb lattice we prove that this crossing mass has power $-1/4$, with upper and lower bounds by fixed positive constants. Our proof also determines the first displacement moment of paths that return to the starting side. This second observable provides the route to the crossing estimate.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The model and the result

<a id="eq:critical-weight"></a>
We use the honeycomb lattice dual to the equilateral triangular tiling of side length one, with one triangular edge direction horizontal. The separation of consecutive horizontal band boundaries is $d_0=\sqrt3/2$. A *port* is the midpoint of an edge on the boundary of a union of triangles. A path between distinct ports follows dual edges, including the two terminal half-edges, visits each dual vertex at most once, and meets the boundary only at its endpoints. Its length $|\gamma|$ is the number of visited dual vertices; ports carry no weight. We assign the critical weight $\rho^{|\gamma|}$, where $$\label{eq:critical-weight}
 \rho=\frac1{\sqrt{2+\sqrt2}},\qquad c=\cos(3\pi/8).$$ This vertex convention agrees with the usual edge convention for the connective constant up to the fixed endpoint factor. Duminil-Copin and Smirnov proved that the reciprocal of $\rho$ is the honeycomb connective constant <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[DCS2012](#ref-DCS2012)].

<a id="eq:01-strip-quantities"></a>
Let $\mathcal S_N$ be the infinite horizontal strip of $N$ triangular bands, where $N\ge1$. Index its bottom ports consecutively by $\mathbb Z$, fix the source at port $0$, and write $Z_D(a,b)$ for the sum of critical weights of paths from $a$ to $b$ in $D$. A path returning to the bottom is called an *arch*; a path ending at the top is called a *bridge*. In both cases all other points of the path are in the interior of the strip. Define $$\label{eq:01-strip-quantities}
 \begin{split}
 K_N(k)&=Z_{\mathcal S_N}(0,k),\\
 \mathcal A_N&=\sum_{k\ne0}K_N(k),\qquad
 \mathcal B_N=\sum_{b\text{ on the top}}Z_{\mathcal S_N}(0,b),\\
 m_N&=\sum_{k\ge1}kK_N(k).
 \end{split}$$ The sums run over actual admissible boundary ports. Adjacent bottom ports have horizontal separation one, so $m_N$ is the first rightward horizontal-displacement moment. Top ports lie on the translated lattice $N/2+\mathbb Z$. These are unnormalized masses: for example the bridge probability measure would divide each bridge weight by $\mathcal B_N$. Figure <a href="#fig:model-conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:model-conventions">1</a> shows the convention.

<figure id="fig:model-conventions">

<figcaption>Port and length conventions. The path passes through six adjacent triangles of the triangular tiling, so it visits six vertices of the honeycomb dual and has port weight $ρ^{6}$. Its two terminal pieces are half-edges. An ordinary vertex-to-vertex path counts full edges instead. The shaded region is one triangular band.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="stmt-thm-strip"></a>
<div id="thm:strip" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Critical strip mass). *For every integer $N\ge1$, all sums in <a href="#eq:01-strip-quantities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:01-strip-quantities">[eq:01-strip-quantities]</a> are finite. With constants independent of $N$, <a id="eq:01-strip-result"></a>$$\label{eq:01-strip-result}
 c\mathcal A_N+\mathcal B_N=1,\qquad
 m_{N+1}-m_N\asymp\mathcal B_N,\qquad
 m_N\asymp N^{3/4},\qquad
 \mathcal B_N\asymp N^{-1/4}.$$ Moreover, $\mathcal B_N$ is nonincreasing in $N$.*

</div>

Here $f_N\asymp g_N$ means that their ratio lies between two fixed positive constants. Thus the theorem determines a bounded-factor power law, rather than only a logarithmic exponent. If a height-zero term is useful, we set $\mathcal B_0=1$ by convention; no strip path is being added to the definition above. The crossing estimate can then be written $\mathcal B_N\asymp(1+N)^{-1/4}$ for $N\ge0$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Earlier work

The two-dimensional polymer exponents arose from the dilute $O(n)$ model and its Coulomb-gas analysis. Nienhuis predicted the honeycomb critical point and the self-avoiding-walk exponents at $n=0$ <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Nienhuis1982](#ref-Nienhuis1982)]. Lawler, Schramm and Werner developed the conjectural conformally invariant scaling picture for planar self-avoiding walk <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[LawlerSchrammWerner2004](#ref-LawlerSchrammWerner2004), Sections 3.3.1, 3.4.3 and 4.1]. Their boundary exponent $5/8$ predicts a boundary-to-boundary mass of power $-5/4$. Summing the endpoint along the opposite side of a strip then predicts the crossing power $-1/4$; this deduction is also stated explicitly in <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DCS2012](#ref-DCS2012), Section 4].

Duminil-Copin and Smirnov proved the critical-point prediction using a parafermionic observable whose local cancellation gives a positive boundary identity <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[DCS2012](#ref-DCS2012), Lemmas 1 and 2]. Their strip argument gives bounds of order $1/N$ and $1$ for the crossing mass. These bounds leave the predicted power undetermined.

The decay of this mass was subsequently proved by Beaton, Bousquet-Mélou, de Gier, Duminil-Copin and Guttmann in their study of surface adsorption <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BeatonEtAl2014](#ref-BeatonEtAl2014), Theorem 10]. Glazman and Manolescu obtained a shorter proof, a logarithmic bound along a sequence of heights, and invariance of the half-plane boundary two-point function under columnwise rhombic deformations with angles in $[\pi/3,2\pi/3]$ <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GM2019](#ref-GM2019), Proposition 1.1 and Theorems 1–2]. Krachun and Panagiotis proved a polynomial upper bound $\mathcal B_N\le100N^{-10^{-10}}$ in their strip convention, together with quantitative sub-ballisticity <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[KrachunPanagiotis2026](#ref-KrachunPanagiotis2026), Theorems 1–2]. Theorem <a href="#thm:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:strip">1</a> establishes the predicted strip power with uniform multiplicative constants in the port convention above. The exponent $3/4$ for $m_N$ describes an unnormalized displacement moment summed over all path lengths, with strip height as its scale. The fixed-length displacement conjecture and the conjectured conformal scaling limit concern different observables.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The boundary identity alone does not determine this power. Our route is to calculate $m_N$ first and then recover $\mathcal B_N$ from its increments. The argument passes from finite strip transfers to a polynomial formula, then to a positive integral that can be estimated uniformly in the height.

In Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">2</a>, cancellation of closed excursions gives the boundary identity and controls finite-height transfer matrices. We introduce rhombic weights with one spectral parameter in each row. A cut between columns records vacant positions and occupied positions paired by path pieces on one side of the cut. The stationary vacuum vector records the weighted sums of these partial pairings with no exterior source; a source vector has one additional strand connected to a specified boundary source. Their local relations belong to the dilute-loop integrable structure; the relation with discrete parafermions was developed by Ikhlef and Cardy, and the weighted-walk normalization and corrected general formulas were treated by Glazman <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[IkhlefCardy2009](#ref-IkhlefCardy2009), [Glazman2015](#ref-Glazman2015)]. We prove the exact local identities used here in Appendix <a href="#app:local-calculations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:local-calculations">7</a>.

Section <a href="#sec:vacuum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:vacuum">3</a> constructs a scalar Pfaffian $p_N$ that turns the stationary vacuum into a vector of bounded-degree polynomials. A degree bound is essential: it makes interpolation an identity argument. Exchange relations and reductions at special parameters have this role in earlier dense and dilute loop calculations <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DiFrancescoZinnJustin2005](#ref-DiFrancescoZinnJustin2005), [GarbaliNienhuis2017Ground](#ref-GarbaliNienhuis2017Ground), [GarbaliNienhuis2017Sum](#ref-GarbaliNienhuis2017Sum)]. Here the degree and stationarity arguments are proved for zero loop weight. Section <a href="#sec:arch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arch">4</a> cuts an arch at every column between its endpoints. This converts $m_N$ into a pairing of source vectors and then into one coefficient of $p_{N+1}/p_N$. A stepped-boundary flux identity proves $m_{N+1}-m_N\asymp\mathcal B_N$.

Section <a href="#sec:integral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:integral">5</a> converts the homogeneous polynomial problem into a positive integral over $0<y_1<\cdots<y_N<1$, with density proportional to $$\prod_{i=1}^N\omega(y_i)
 \prod_{i<j}\frac{(y_j-y_i)^2}{y_i+y_j},\qquad
 \omega(y)=\frac12\sqrt{\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{2y}}-1}.$$ Positivity of a Cauchy-moment determinant makes the coincident-parameter limit nonsingular. The pair interaction in the resulting integral is the one appearing in generalized Bures ensembles <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ForresterKieburg2016](#ref-ForresterKieburg2016)]. The derivation from the strip polynomial fixes the normalization and identifies the integral with the arch moment. Finally, Section <a href="#sec:hard-edge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hard-edge">6</a> compares this measure with Bures–Laguerre laws. Schur’s Pfaffian identity and de Bruijn’s integration formula give their normalizing constants; positive association gives the comparison. The smallest-coordinate estimates yield $m_N\asymp N^{3/4}$. Monotonicity and the increment identity then give the crossing power in Theorem <a href="#thm:strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:strip">1</a>.

<a id="sec:flux"></a>
### Positive flux and finite strip transfers

We write <a id="eq:01-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:01-constants}
 t=\frac38,\qquad \lambda=\frac\pi8,\qquad
 d=\cos(2\lambda)=\sin(2\lambda),\qquad C=\cos\lambda.$$ Then $c=\cos(3\lambda)$ and $\rho=C-c=1/(2C)$, consistently with <a href="#eq:critical-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-weight">[eq:critical-weight]</a>.

The boundary identity controls positive path sums before any spectral parameter is introduced. We use it first to make the fixed-height transfer construction convergent. The local identities then allow the stationary vector to be recovered by polynomial interpolation.

<a id="stmt-lem-boundary-flux"></a>
<div id="lem:boundary-flux" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:01-boundary-identity"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Boundary identity). *Let $D$ be a finite simply connected union of triangles with simple polygonal boundary, and let $a$ be a boundary port. If $W(\gamma)$ is the total signed turning of a path, measured from its inward initial direction to its outward final direction, then $$\label{eq:01-boundary-identity}
 \sum_{b\in\partial D}\ \sum_{\gamma:a\to b}
       \rho^{|\gamma|}e^{itW(\gamma)}=1.$$ If $D$ is convex, its unsigned boundary partition sum is at most $1/c$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-boundary-flux"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-boundary-flux</summary>

*Proof.* Send mass one along the inward half-edge at $a$. At each newly visited vertex split it between the two possible continuations, multiplying by $\rho e^{i\lambda}$ and $\rho e^{-i\lambda}$, respectively. The identity $2\rho\cos\lambda=1$ conserves total mass. Stop a branch when it exits $D$ or is about to enter a vertex already visited. This produces a finite tree because $D$ has finitely many vertices.

For a stopped collision, the newly closed cycle is simple and has a disjoint incoming stem. The stem lies outside the cycle: it starts on $\partial D$ and cannot cross the cycle before their first common vertex. Consequently the unused branch at that vertex is exterior to the cycle. The cycle turn there is $\pi/3$ in the counterclockwise orientation, rather than $-\pi/3$; the clockwise statement is reversed. The turn from the stem into the cycle replaces this closing turn by its negative. The two ways to traverse the cycle therefore have winding increments $4\pi/3$ and $-4\pi/3$ relative to the stem. They have the same unsigned weight and cancel, since $e^{it(4\pi/3)}+e^{-it(4\pi/3)}=0$. Cycle reversal is an involution on all stopped collisions. The remaining terminal mass gives <a href="#eq:01-boundary-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:01-boundary-identity">[eq:01-boundary-identity]</a>.

For a path ending at $b$, close it using the counterclockwise boundary arc from $b$ to $a$. The turns at the two ports are $\pi/2$, so the turning theorem for this simple closed curve gives $$W(\gamma)=\pi-\operatorname{turn}_{\partial D}(b\to a).$$ For a convex boundary the latter turn lies in $[0,2\pi]$. Thus $|W(\gamma)|\le\pi$ and $\cos(tW(\gamma))\ge\cos(3\pi/8)=c$. Taking real parts proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:01-rhombic-weights"></a>
We next introduce auxiliary spectral parameters. A column has $N$ rhombi stacked along horizontal edges; in row $i$ the other edge makes angle $u_i/t$ with the horizontal. The same tiles make sense as combinatorial diagrams for complex $u_i$. Their four ports carry noncrossing partial pairings. Port occupancies must agree when tiles are glued, and every closed component has weight zero. Put $$\label{eq:01-rhombic-weights}
\begin{aligned}
 a(u)&=\cos(2u-3\lambda),&
 L(u)&=\sin(2\lambda+u)\sin(3\lambda+u)=\frac{C+a(u)}2,\\
 A(u)&=\frac{d\sin(3\lambda-u)}{L(u)},&
 U(u)&=\frac{d\sin u}{L(u)},\\
 B(u)&=\frac{\sin u\sin(3\lambda-u)}{L(u)}
       =\frac{a(u)-c}{a(u)+C},\\
 E(u)&=\frac{\sin(3\lambda-u)\sin(2\lambda-u)}{L(u)},&
 F(u)&=\frac{\sin(u-\lambda)\sin u}{L(u)}.
\end{aligned}$$ These weights come from Nienhuis’s integrable $O(n)$ construction <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Nienhuis1990CriticalMulticritical](#ref-Nienhuis1990CriticalMulticritical)], in the self-avoiding-walk normalization displayed in <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GM2019](#ref-GM2019)]; the general parafermionic and integrable local formulas are discussed in <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[IkhlefCardy2009](#ref-IkhlefCardy2009), [Glazman2015](#ref-Glazman2015)]. The empty tile has weight one. A single connection between opposite ports has weight $B$. A bottom–left or top–right turn has weight $A$; the other two turns have weight $U$. The configurations with two turns of the first or second type have weights $E$ and $F$, respectively. At $u=\lambda$ or $2\lambda$, splitting along the short diagonal recovers two triangular tiles, with weight $\rho$ per visited triangle. At $u=0$ or $3\lambda$ the row is flat: each route continues deterministically through the adjacent left or right port, and the two disjoint routes can coexist with weight one.

The dilute diagram algebra and its Yang–Baxter operators have an algebraic construction in Grimm and Pearce <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GrimmPearce1993](#ref-GrimmPearce1993)]. Their distinction between gauge-invariant periodic partition functions and other gauge-dependent quantities is relevant here: we specify the caps, scalar factors and port ordering, and prove the identities for these choices.

We use the planar diagram algebra whose inputs and outputs are ordered slots, each vacant or occupied. Composition glues matching slots and annihilates a mismatch or a closed loop. Tensor product means juxtaposition; transpose reflects a diagram, interchanging inputs and outputs. The two-slot operator $R(v)$ has coefficients $1,A(v),B(v),U(v),E(v),F(v)$ on, respectively, the empty diagram, a single vertical connection, a single diagonal connection, a cap alone or cup alone, two vertical connections, and a cap together with a cup. There are two choices for a single vertical or diagonal connection, as shown in Figure <a href="#fig:local-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:local-operator">2</a>.

<figure id="fig:local-operator">

<figcaption>The nine local diagrams of $R(v)$, with coefficients evaluated at $v$. Grey dots mark available slots; a slot is occupied exactly when a black strand meets it. Gluing requires matching occupancies and assigns zero to every closed component. These are operator diagrams, rather than a geometric drawing of a rhombus.</figcaption>
</figure>

- Tile 2: A vertical black strand on the left connecting bottom-left to top-left; right slots marked with grey dots; labeled "A".
- Tile 3: A vertical black strand on the right connecting bottom-right to top-right; left slots marked with grey dots; labeled "A".
- Tile 4: A diagonal black strand connecting bottom-left to top-right; bottom-right and top-left marked with grey dots; labeled "B".
- Tile 5: A diagonal black strand connecting bottom-right to top-left; bottom-left and top-right marked with grey dots; labeled "B".
Bottom row:
- Tile 6: A cup (U-turn) connecting bottom-left to bottom-right; top slots marked with grey dots; labeled "U".
- Tile 7: A cap (inverted U-turn) connecting top-left to top-right; bottom slots marked with grey dots; labeled "U".
- Tile 8: Two vertical black strands connecting bottom-left to top-left and bottom-right to top-right; labeled "E".

To the right of the bottom row, text with a bracket reads: "inputs below, outputs above".

Number the rows from bottom to top. A column swept from left to right uses $R(3\lambda-u_i)$ in row $i$, with local ordered inputs (south, west) and outputs (east, north). The north output of row $i$ is the south input of row $i+1$. The geometric and operator weights agree because $$A(3\lambda-u)=U(u),\quad U(3\lambda-u)=A(u),\quad
 B(3\lambda-u)=B(u),\quad E(3\lambda-u)=F(u).$$ The last relation also gives $F(3\lambda-u)=E(u)$. Figure <a href="#fig:rhombus-dictionary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:rhombus-dictionary">3</a> records the port order.

<figure id="fig:rhombus-dictionary">

<figcaption>From a geometric rhombus to a column operator. South and west are the ordered inputs; east and north are the ordered outputs. The operator is drawn with its inputs below and outputs above, so the rhombic parameter $u$ corresponds to the operator argument $3λ − u$. The rhombus is schematic; only the angle label and port order matter.</figcaption>
</figure>

Define $S_2$, from one slot to two, by sending a vacancy to a vacancy plus $\rho$ times a cup, and an occupied strand to either output slot with coefficient $\rho$. Define $S_3$, from no slots to two, to be the sum of the vacant diagram and a cup.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-identities"></a>
<div id="lem:local-identities" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:01-braid"></a>
<a id="eq:01-factorization"></a>
<a id="eq:01-splitting"></a>
<a id="eq:01-unitarity"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Local diagram identities). *Writing $R_i$ for action in slots $i,i+1$, one has $$\begin{aligned}
 R_2(y)R_1(x+y)R_2(x)&=R_1(x)R_2(x+y)R_1(y),
       \label{eq:01-braid}\\
 R(s\lambda)&=S_sS_s^{\mathsf t},\qquad s=2,3,
       \label{eq:01-factorization}\\
 R_2(x)R_1(x+s\lambda)(I\otimes S_s)
       &=(S_s\otimes I)\widehat R_s(x),
       \label{eq:01-splitting}\\
 R(x)R(-x)&=I.\label{eq:01-unitarity}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\widehat R_2(x)=R(x+\lambda)$ and $\widehat R_3(x)$ is the one-slot identity. These are identities of rational functions. They can be specialized wherever the relevant sides are regular, including at removable singularities; an individual operator at a genuine pole is not assigned a finite value.*

</div>

The complete coefficient proof is in Appendix <a href="#app:local-calculations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:local-calculations">7</a>.

For a cut between two columns of $N$ rows, number its slots from bottom to top. Let $\mathcal L_N^0$ be the vector space with basis the noncrossing partial pairings of occupied slots; all other slots are vacant. Let $\mathcal L_N^1$ have the same pairings with one additional unpaired occupied slot connected to an exterior source. This defect cannot be enclosed by a pair. Restricting a single path to one side of a cut can produce several paired fragments as well as the source-connected strand, as Figure <a href="#fig:cut-states" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cut-states">4</a> illustrates.

<figure id="fig:cut-states">

<figcaption>A single arch may cross a cut several times. In this schematic example the left half has a source strand at slot $1$ and pairs $(2,3)$, $(5,6)$; the right half has pairs $(1,2)$, $(3,5)$ and a source strand at slot $6$. Slot $4$ is vacant on both sides. Each source strand is exposed, meaning that no pair encloses its slot. Gluing the two states recovers one path from $a$ to $b$, with no closed component.</figcaption>
</figure>
